EXPERIMENT: One-visible-assertion resampling on the first twenty harder synthetic tasks

Purpose. Correct the earlier synthetic comparison by exposing one assertion, as the original MBPP training prompt does, then compare task-level pass coverage with the twenty original tasks summarized from the saved `c4fthzd3` training trace.

Setup. Used the same first twenty rows from `analysis/mbpp_category_synthetic_tasks_harder100.jsonl` and generated sixteen new completions per task with base `Qwen/Qwen2.5-Coder-0.5B-Instruct`, no adapter, temperature 1.0, top-p 1.0, seed 42, and maximum 2,048 new tokens. Prompt construction used `data.build_qwen_evalplus_prompt` with the first generated assertion visible in the code docstring, matching the repository's original MBPP prompt path. All three assertions were scored with `sandbox.score_completion` and a 3-second timeout. The complete prompts' generated continuations and per-completion scores are saved in `analysis/mbpp_category_synthetic_harder20_16gen_visible1_eval.json`; the evaluator is `analysis/evaluate_mbpp_harder20_16gen.py` with `--visible-test-count 1`.

Synthetic results. Of 20 tasks, 18 had no full-pass completion, none had all sixteen pass, and 2 had mixed full-pass outcomes. Two of 320 completions (0.63%) passed all three assertions. Across 960 expected assertions, 148 passed (15.42%). Counting whether at least one completion reached each exact score, exactly zero, one, two, and three assertions were reached on 20/20 (100%), 16/20 (80%), 7/20 (35%), and 2/20 tasks (10%). By cumulative threshold, at least one completion passed one or more assertions on 17/20 (85%), at least two on 8/20 (40%), and all three on 2/20 (10%).

Corrected side-by-side task coverage. On the original tasks, the saved trace groups showed exact-score coverage of 19/20, 7/20, 6/20, and 11/20 for exactly zero through three assertions, and cumulative coverage of 20/20, 15/20, 15/20, and 11/20 for at least zero through three. On the one-visible-assertion synthetic tasks, the corresponding counts were 20/20, 16/20, 7/20, and 2/20 exact, and 20/20, 17/20, 8/20, and 2/20 cumulative. The original samples came from the evolving `c4fthzd3` run, with first occurrences across trace rows 560 to 10,352, while synthetic samples came from the base model at one fixed checkpoint. The original and synthetic task subsets also differ. These results show the corrected synthetic prompt still has markedly fewer fully passing groups, but they do not isolate task difficulty from model training state or task selection.

Environment. Python 3.12.3, PyTorch 2.8.0+cu128, vLLM 0.10.2, NVIDIA RTX A5000 with 24,112 MiB reported memory. Source code before this evaluator update was commit `bda5393`. Limitations include one seed, twenty tasks per set, generated assertions whose reference and tests were created together, and the mismatch between a fixed base model and original-task generations sampled across training updates. This EvalPlus-style evaluation is not the official EvalPlus benchmark.

Experiment: Saved rollout groups on the first 100 original MBPP training tasks.

Purpose and selection. Measure the task-level distribution of existing sixteen-generation groups on the first 100 rows of the original MBPP training pool after the canonical EvalPlus exclusion. The rows are in repository dataset order and have IDs 601, 609, 613, 617, 621, 625, 627, 634, 636, 640, 642, 645, 646, 647, 648, 649, 650, 651, 652, 653, 654, 655, 656, 657, 658, 659, 660, 661, 662, 663, 664, 665, 666, 667, 668, 669, 670, 671, 672, 673, 674, 675, 676, 677, 678, 679, 680, 681, 682, 683, 684, 685, 686, 687, 688, 689, 690, 691, 692, 693, 694, 695, 696, 697, 698, 699, 700, 701, 702, 703, 704, 705, 706, 707, 708, 709, 710, 711, 712, 713, 714, 715, 716, 717, 718, 719, 727, 729, 738, 746, 747, 756, 761, 768, 774, 776, 779, 783, 789, and 795.

Method. For each task, selected the earliest complete sixteen-candidate group by trace order from W&B run `c4fthzd3`'s local `outputs/grpo-mbpp-fulltrain-8x16-b128-micro2-workers8-0.5b-50000-dual-greedy-evalplus031-visible1-save10-restart-20260929/reward-trace.jsonl`. This selection used task ID and trace order only, without inspecting correctness. Each task has three assertions. The prompt exposed one assertion; the scorer checked all three. The run used the hybrid reward with coefficient 0.75. First-occurrence trace offsets ranged from 64 to 8,867. These are early rollout groups from a changing training run, not samples from a single fixed checkpoint. The per-task reward and pass-count summaries are in `analysis/mbpp_original100_saved16gen_eval.json`.

Results. Across tasks, at least one generation reached exactly zero, one, two, or three passed assertions on 96/100 (96%), 36/100 (36%), 21/100 (21%), and 68/100 (68%) tasks. Cumulative task coverage was 100/100 with at least zero, 84/100 with at least one, 80/100 with at least two, and 68/100 with all three. Across 1,600 completions, 885 passed zero tests, 140 passed one, 84 passed two, and 491 passed all three. Hybrid reward varied within 81/100 groups (81%); 19 groups (19%) had flat reward. Mean within-group reward standard deviation was 0.2500.

Limitations. The candidate source is an evolving GRPO run, so task groups have different model states. This is not a fixed-base comparison with the harder synthetic evaluation. The first 100 filtered rows and the first 100 harder synthetic rows are different task subsets. The original trace is ignored local output; the compact tracked summary preserves task IDs, candidate scores, reward values, and first trace offsets but not full candidate text. Ground-truth labels were used only as saved by the original run to score candidates after trace-order selection.

============================================================================
EXPERIMENT: One-visible-assertion 16-generation comparison on 100 original and 100 harder synthetic tasks

Purpose. Measure how often a group of sixteen completions produces zero, partial, or full assertion reward on original MBPP tasks and on the harder synthetic tasks. One assertion was visible in each prompt, and all three assertions were scored.

Original set. Used the first 100 original MBPP training-pool rows after canonical EvalPlus exclusion, in repository dataset order. For each task, selected its earliest complete sixteen-candidate group from the saved `c4fthzd3` training trace by task ID and trace order, without inspecting correctness. These original candidates are from the evolving training run, with first occurrences at trace rows 64 to 8,867. The compact saved summary is `analysis/mbpp_original100_saved16gen_eval.json`.

Synthetic set. Used the first 100 rows of `analysis/mbpp_category_synthetic_tasks_harder100.jsonl`. Generated 16 completions per task from the base `Qwen/Qwen2.5-Coder-0.5B-Instruct` model without an adapter, at temperature 1.0, top-p 1.0, seed 42, and a 2,048-token limit. Prompt construction used `data.build_qwen_evalplus_prompt` with the first assertion visible. Scored all three assertions through the local sandbox and recorded the run's configured hybrid reward: 0.75 times full-pass plus 0.25 times passed-test fraction. The generated completions and per-candidate scores are saved in `analysis/mbpp_category_synthetic_harder100_16gen_visible1_eval.json`. The evaluator is `analysis/evaluate_mbpp_harder20_16gen.py`, which accepts `--task-count 100` and `--visible-test-count 1`.

Task-level score coverage. Rows below count tasks with at least one completion reaching each exact score; rows overlap. Original versus synthetic counts were exactly zero tests, 96/100 (96%) versus 100/100 (100%); exactly one, 36/100 (36%) versus 77/100 (77%); exactly two, 21/100 (21%) versus 32/100 (32%); and exactly three, 68/100 (68%) versus 17/100 (17%). By cumulative threshold, original versus synthetic coverage was at least zero tests, 100/100 versus 100/100; at least one, 84/100 (84%) versus 83/100 (83%); at least two, 80/100 (80%) versus 40/100 (40%); and all three, 68/100 (68%) versus 17/100 (17%).

Group outcomes and signal. By full-task success, original groups were none 32/100 (32%), all sixteen 3/100 (3%), and mixed 65/100 (65%). Synthetic groups were none 83/100 (83%), all sixteen 0/100, and mixed 17/100 (17%). Across all 1,600 completions per set, original passed-test counts were zero 885, one 140, two 84, and three 491; synthetic counts were zero 981, one 490, two 86, and three 43. Under the configured hybrid reward, the synthetic reward varied in 83/100 groups (83%) and was flat zero in 17/100. Original reward varied in 81/100 groups (81%); 16 groups were flat zero and 3 were flat at reward one because all sixteen generations passed. Mean group reward standard deviation was 0.0805 synthetic and 0.2500 original. A one-test-only completion receives reward 1/12 (about 0.083); two tests receive 1/6 (about 0.167); a full pass receives 1.0.

Interpretation. The synthetic tasks are much less likely to produce a full solution under these samples, but their reward is not sparse in the binary sense: 83% of groups have within-group reward variation and partial passing is common. This suggests useful GRPO reward signal for most groups, while 17% of synthetic groups are flat zero and contribute no task-reward preference signal. The signal is weaker in scale and depth than on the original groups, as shown by the lower mean reward standard deviation and much lower two-test and full-pass coverage. This does not prove training will improve held-out evaluation.

Limitations. The original groups come from different points in an evolving training run, while the synthetic groups were generated from a fixed base model. The task sets are not paired, and the first 100 rows have their own category mix. Both use one visible assertion and score three assertions, but this remains a diagnostic of the sampled group reward, not official EvalPlus evaluation. Synthetic references and tests were generated together and are not independently validated. Environment for synthetic generation and scoring: Python 3.12.3, PyTorch 2.8.0+cu128, vLLM 0.10.2, NVIDIA RTX A5000 with 24,112 MiB reported memory. Synthetic evaluator source commit before final metadata correction: `f3a49b0`; original run source commit: `b4db7dca2bbae4886cae81f9d6d14c1ddaf1e28a`.

============================================================================
ANALYSIS: Why the first 100 harder synthetic tasks are harder than the first 100 original MBPP tasks

Purpose and selection. Inspect the actual original and harder synthetic task definitions, not only their pass rates. The original set is the first 100 records in the filtered MBPP training pool, identified by `analysis/mbpp_original100_saved16gen_eval.json`. The synthetic set is the first 100 rows in `analysis/mbpp_category_synthetic_tasks_harder100.jsonl`. The harder prompt inputs and their three example task IDs are in `analysis/mbpp_category_api_prompts_harder100.jsonl`. Original descriptions, references, and tests were read from the local Hugging Face MBPP Arrow cache by task ID. No candidates or task rows were selected using correctness labels.

Descriptive code and specification measures. Original versus synthetic prompt word count mean (median) was 15.1 (14) versus 103.2 (95.5), about 6.8 times as many words. Reference solutions had mean (median) 6.9 (5.5) versus 20.6 (19) nonblank lines, and 52.9 (37.5) versus 186.7 (164.5) Python AST nodes. A loop appeared in 39 versus 91 tasks; a branch appeared in 48 versus 97; nested loop/control-flow depth of at least two appeared in 8 versus 43. Mean loop nodes were 0.6 versus 2.0, and mean branch nodes were 0.8 versus 4.1. The number of functions per reference was similar at about 1.1 in both sets, so the extra structure is mainly inside the task logic. These are descriptive complexity proxies, not direct measures of human difficulty.

Comparison with prompt examples. For 93/100 generated tasks, reference AST size exceeded the median AST size of its three source examples; for 81/100, it exceeded all three. Generated references had more loop nodes than the median source example in 75/100 tasks and more branch nodes in 83/100. These results support the prompt instruction producing more involved code than the examples, but longer reference code can reflect verbosity and generated-oracle choices as well as problem difficulty.

What adds difficulty. Many tasks require several dependent operations and precise ordering. For example, `01-001` computes tiered energy charges, handles consumption above the last numeric band, adds a fixed fee, applies tax, and rounds the result. `06-010` parses quoted comma-separated fields, handles quotes inside quoted fields, and rejects invalid text after a closing quote. `09-006` counts subsets by sum and size while treating equal values at distinct positions separately and reducing modulo a constant. Geometry tasks require polygon clipping or rectangle optimization for concave shapes, and the graph task `12-001` asks for a shortest Hamiltonian path. Many specs add tie rules, boundary conditions, invalid-input behavior, or return conventions that the short original descriptions do not state.

Counterevidence and quality risks. The synthetic descriptions are much more verbose, and some of that length restates assumptions rather than adding useful challenge. Semantic patterns repeat despite unique surface descriptions: `02-002` and `02-004` are near-duplicate divisor-count searches; `11-001` and `11-004` both involve polygon clipping; `11-003` and `11-005` both involve rectangle-union area; and several ranking tasks repeat top-k or nearest-median patterns. Eight entry-point names recur, with a maximum frequency of four. Some tasks lack feasibility bounds: `12-001` is NP-hard for unbounded n, while advanced polygon optimization and divisor-count searches also omit practical size limits. At least one spec, `03-010`, has conflicting reversal rules for a short final block. Excessive complexity, repeated templates, and ambiguous or infeasible requirements can make training harder without improving useful transfer.

Category and sampling caveats. The two task sets span the same thirteen broad categories with broadly similar counts, but are unpaired: for example, the original first 100 contain six arithmetic and six search/sort tasks, while the synthetic first 100 contain six arithmetic and twelve search/sort tasks. Category-matched median reference AST size is higher for synthetic tasks in each category in this sample, but some strata contain very few rows. The first 100 original rows are determined by source dataset order; they are not a random sample. All complexity measures use generated references and do not establish semantic correctness or independent test coverage. The synthetic prompt, generated reference, and three tests were created together. These findings explain why the sampled synthetic set is harder for the base model, but they do not prove that this difficulty will improve EvalPlus transfer.

============================================================================
EXPERIMENT: Category-proportional 10x harder MBPP synthetic-data generation

Purpose. Generate 5,319 harder synthetic tasks to combine with the 591-task original MBPP training pool for 5,910 total rows. Preserve the original category distribution and keep the earlier easier dataset intact.

Setup and generation. Used `analysis/generate_mbpp_category_prompts.py --total-prompts 5319 --harder-than-examples --seed 20261009` and GPT-6 Luna through `analysis/generate_mbpp_category_tasks_api.py`, with reasoning effort `none`, 20 concurrent requests, three literal calls per task, and exact normalized-description deduplication. The first API pass and one retry pass left a small number of malformed JSON responses. A second retry with eleven attempts per failed prompt reused saved validated state. All 5,319 tasks passed reference execution and have three generated assertions. The complete usage ledger includes all retries and replacements.

Category allocation. Arithmetic 342; number theory 702; lists 558; search/sort 612; counting 450; strings 693; sets 180; mappings 450; recurrence/DP 468; matrices 54; geometry 342; graphs 54; predicates 414. Each count is exactly nine times the matching original category count.

Cost and time. The generator made 6,574 API responses, using 4,142,031 input tokens, 73,967 cached input tokens, and 3,602,069 output tokens. At the recorded GPT-6 Luna rates, estimated cost was $2.208581. Active generator command time across the three attempts totaled 2,920.662 seconds, or 48 minutes 40.7 seconds; end-to-end wall time was about 54 minutes. Detailed accounting and hashes are in `analysis/mbpp_harder5319_generation_summary.json`.

Preflight and training instrumentation. The matched config loaded 5,910 training rows and 378 EvalPlus rows with one visible assertion, using a fresh HF dataset cache after the default cache exposed a zero-byte parquet. Added W&B per-step curves for the mean fraction of task generations passing at least one, two, and three tests. Per-task counts and fractions are written to the training output's `task-coverage-trace.jsonl` with task ID and optimizer step. Targeted training and sandbox tests passed: 28 passed. The user directed that the separate 16-generation base-model evaluation be skipped.

Selection and limitations. All generated tasks were kept after exact normalized-description deduplication; no ground-truth outputs were used to select tasks. Each generated reference and its three assertions were produced together, so the assertions are not an independent oracle. Exact text uniqueness does not rule out semantic repetition. No training checkpoint has been selected using EvalPlus results; checkpoint selection is disabled.

One-time prelaunch cleanup. Before training, removed 65 older checkpoints, steps 10 through 650, from the inactive easier-data run. Verified and retained its latest complete checkpoint at step 660. This reclaimed 7,940,414,189 logical bytes (7.395 GiB). No checkpoints will be deleted after the harder-data run starts.

Training launch. Started the separate harder-data run from the base model at 2026-10-09 07:46:46 UTC with W&B run ID `mbpphard10x1009` in `kunal-personal/grpo-mbpp`. The configuration preserves the linked run's optimizer, batch sizes, GRPO reward, seed, 50,000-step target, and save/evaluation cadence; it changes the synthetic data path and run identity. The ten-minute supervisor is running as PID 34560. On failure it records a diagnosis and resumes from the latest complete checkpoint in the new output directory. No checkpoint cleanup will occur after launch. W&B URL: https://wandb.ai/kunal-personal/grpo-mbpp/runs/mbpphard10x1009.
